We have STLC with CBV, locally nameless. We need strong normalization for closed well-typed terms.

- For base type Bool: SN t
- For arrow T1 -> T2: for all u, value u -> ...

Actually, with strong normalization for CBV, we need the relation to be:
```
R(Bool) t := SN t  (or SN t and ...)
R(T1 -> T2) t := SN t /\ forall u, R(T1) u -> R(T2) (t u)   -- for CBV? 
```

Hmm, for CBV, the usual logical relation for strong normalization is defined with:
- R(Bool) = SN
- R(A→B) = { t | SN t ∧ ∀u ∈ R(A), t u ∈ R(B) }

Actually with CBV, we need the "hereditary" property. Since in CBV, an abstraction is a value and doesn't reduce until applied to a value. Let's recall standard reference: Pierce's TAPL uses a different approach. For strong normalization with call-by-value, the standard logical relation is:

R(Bool) = SN
R(A→B) = SN ∩ { t | ∀u, value u → u ∈ R(A) → t u ∈ R(B) }

But we also need to handle free variables. The typing judgment uses de Bruijn *free* variables with named contexts, and substitutions. The fundamental theorem approach: prove by induction on typing derivation typically requires a substitution lemma for the context.

Since the language is locally nameless with free variables and contexts, the standard approach: define interpretation of contexts, and prove:

1. If Γ ⊢ t : T and γ ∈ ⟦Γ⟧ then t[γ] ∈ ⟦T⟧. Hmm, but substituting for free variables.

- T_Var: Γ x = Some T → R T (γ x). ✓

- T_App: induction: t1[γ] ∈ R(T1→T2), t2[γ] ∈ R(T1). Need to show t1[γ] t2[γ] ∈ R(T2).

Actually the standard locally-nameless approach for the fundamental theorem: Interpret contexts where each γ x is a *value* in the relation. Actually, let me just carefully develop the proof myself; it's a standard Tait-style argument. Let me handle CBV specifics.

Let me define things concretely. First, define free variable substitution:

```
Fixpoint subst (gamma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => gamma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst gamma t1) (subst gamma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst gamma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst gamma t1) (subst gamma t2) (subst gamma t3)
  end.
```

For γ, we'll use environments that map most atoms to themselves (identity), so that free variables not in the domain remain as fvars. Hmm, but then the interpretation must say: for x not in Γ, γ x = fvar x. Standard: define the environment as a function atom -> tm with identity off-domain. Then the interpretation: for all x, Γ x = Some T -> LR T (γ x). Hmm, for x with Γ x = None, γ x should be fvar x (or anything?). For the T_Abs case, we need to open with a fresh x and extend γ; and the substitution lemma's statement: if Γ ⊢ t : T and γ agrees with Γ in the sense that for all x, Γ x = Some T -> LR T (γ x) and for x not in Γ's domain, γ x = fvar x... Hmm, but we could just require for all x: match Γ x with Some T => LR T (γ x) | None => γ x = fvar x end. That's clean.

Alternatively, define γ as a "list of (atom, tm) bindings" and eval by lookup defaulting to fvar. Simpler to do functions.

Alternative approach avoiding substitution: Since the T_Abs rule uses open with a fresh variable and the typing derivation for body is under extended context, we do need context interpretation and substitution. Standard: use `pick fresh x for L` — we can implement a function to compute a fresh atom: e.g., `fresh (L : list atom) := fold_right max 0 L + 1` or simpler `S (max_list)`; need not in L. There is `Nat.max` etc. Let's define:

```
Fixpoint max_atoms (L : list atom) : atom :=
  match L with
  | [] => 0
  | x :: L' => Nat.max x (max_atoms L')
  end.
Definition fresh (L : list atom) := S (max_atoms L).
Lemma fresh_not_in : forall L, ~ In (fresh L) L.
```

Now the substitution-open lemma. Let's state:

```
Lemma subst_open_rec : forall t gamma k u x,
  gamma x = ... 
```
Hmm, the general lemma: `subst gamma (open_rec k u t) = open_rec k (subst gamma u) (subst gamma t)`. Is that true? Let's check: open_rec replaces bvar k with u (a term possibly containing fvars). subst gamma on bvar k leaves it; hmm. Let's prove it: induction on t:
- tm_bvar i: LHS = subst gamma (if i =? k then u else bvar i) = if i =? k then subst gamma u else bvar i. RHS = open_rec k (subst gamma u) (bvar i) = if i =? k then subst gamma u else bvar i. ✓
- tm_fvar x: LHS = gamma x. RHS = open_rec k (subst gamma u) (gamma x) = subst-open inside gamma x? Wait RHS: open_rec k (subst gamma u) (subst gamma (fvar x)) = open_rec k (subst gamma u) (gamma x). Hmm, this opens inside gamma x! If gamma x contains bvar k, they'd be replaced. LHS = gamma x. So not equal in general, unless gamma x is locally closed (no bvars). Indeed if gamma maps to values which are locally closed, then open_rec on it is identity. So the lemma needs hypothesis: for all x, lc_at 0 (gamma x) — or specifically no bvar issues. Let's state with a hypothesis "forall x, lc_at k (gamma x)" maybe. Hmm: open_rec k u (gamma x) = gamma x when lc_at k (gamma x)? Is that true? lc_at k means all bvars < k. open_rec replaces bvar k only, so bvars < k unchanged, and any bvar >= ... lc_at k says all bvars are < k, so no bvar equal to k, so open_rec k u (gamma x) = gamma x. Yes if the substitution is at index k for term with all bvars < k. But during recursion under binders, k increases.

Hmm, but actually we can avoid this: rather than using substitution of environments into terms with open, note the term we substitute is t[γ] where t is the abstraction body (with bvar 0 under the binder). In the abstraction case, we have the typing derivation for `open t (fvar x)` where x fresh. Then substituting γ' = γ[x:=u]:

`(open t (fvar x))[γ']` =? `open (t[γ]) u` (with x fresh for t, i.e., x ∉ FV t, so γ' and γ agree on FV t except x isn't in it).

Now, the delicate part is LR_back and the structure of the arrow case. Let me think carefully about CBV SN logical relation.

Standard reference: For CBV, the logical relation for SN is often:
```
R(Bool) = SN
R(A→B) = SN ∩ { t | ∀ v. value v -> R(A) v -> R(B) (t v) }
```
And the crucial lemma is: if t ∈ R(A→B) and t --> t', then t' ∈ R(A→B) — requires t' SN (from t SN) and for the application part: (t' v) — since t v --> t' v by ST_App1 requiring lc v, which holds. Then R(B)(t v) → R(B)(t' v) by IH on the type structure (LR_back at type B). So LR_back is proven by induction on the type, requiring: SN t from SN t' and t --> t'. Wait direction: given t --> t' and LR T t', show LR T t. SN t: since t --> t' and SN t'. Need SN_intro: for all t'', t --> t'' -> SN t''. If step were deterministic, t'' = t'. Without determinism, hmm.

Alternative: strengthen LR to include "all one-step reducts are in LR" : 
`LR T t := SN t /\ lc t /\ (forall t', t --> t' -> LR T t') /\ (arrow part)`. That's circular (negative occurrence? `forall t', t --> t' -> LR T t'` — LR appears negatively in the definition of LR at the same type... The Fixpoint is by type structure, so it's fine? Defining Fixpoint LR (T) : tm -> Prop := match T with ... (forall t', t --> t' -> LR T t') ... end. This is a nested occurrence of LR T inside its own definition at the same type, which might not be structurally decreasing for the Fixpoint guard checker. Since it's inside match on T but recursive calls are on T (same), guard checker would reject.

Use the SN induction instead: Since SN is an inductive predicate (accessibility), we can do induction on SN t' ... hmm, SN t' doesn't obviously help with t --> t''.

Actually, determinism of step is quite easy to prove here. Let's do it.

Step rules:
1. ST_AppAbs: lc (abs T t), value v → app (abs T t) v --> open t v.
2. ST_App1: t1 --> t1', lc t2 → app t1 t2 --> app t1' t2.
3. ST_App2: value v1, t2 --> t2' → app v1 t2 --> app v1 t2'.
4. ST_IfTrue: lc t1, lc t2 → if true t1 t2 --> t1.
5. ST_IfFalse: → if false t1 t2 --> t2.
6. ST_If: t1 --> t1', lc t2, lc t3 → if t1 t2 t3 --> if t1' t2 t3.

Determinism proof: Given H1 : t --> t1, H2 : t --> t2, show t1 = t2. By induction on H1 (or on t and inversion). Simplest: `intros t t1 t2 H1; induction H1; intros H2; inversion H2; subst; try reflexivity; ...` The tricky cases: ST_AppAbs vs ST_App2: conclusion app (abs T t) v. ST_App2 requires value v1 where v1 = abs T t (true), and t2 --> t2' where t2 = v, i.e., v --> t2'. But H1's premise is `value v`, so v --> t2' contradicts value_no_step. Need value_no_step lemma: value v -> v --> v' -> False. Prove by induction on value v? value has constructors: v_abs, v_true, v_false. Inversion on step with LHS being abs/true/false: no constructor matches — `inversion H`. Let's do: `intros v Hv; induction Hv; intros v' Hstep; inversion Hstep.` For v_abs case, Hstep : tm_abs T t --> v'; inversion Hstep should solve since all step constructors have LHS tm_app or tm_if. ✓.

Also ST_App1 vs ST_AppAbs: H1 = ST_AppAbs: app (abs) v with value v. H2 = ST_App1: requires (abs T t) --> t1', contradicting value_no_step (abs is a value by v_abs with lc). Need: lc_at 0 (abs T t) → value (abs T t) via v_abs.

ST_App1 vs ST_App2: H2 = ST_App2 with value v1 where v1 = t1 (the function part). H2's step premise: t2 --> t2'. Hmm, in this case H1 = ST_App1 (t1 --> t1', lc t2) and H2 = ST_App2 (value v1 with v1 = t1, t2 --> t2'). Wait conclusions: H2 : app v1 t2 --> app v1 t2' where v1 = t1. Both have LHS app t1 t2. So we need t1 = v1 (yes, same) and get t1' vs t2'. Hmm, H1 says app t1 t2 --> app t1' t2 and H2 says app t1 t2 --> app t1 t2'. For these to be equal we need t1' = t1 and t2' = t2. But H1's premise t1 --> t1' with H2's premise value t1 → contradiction by value_no_step. Great, so contradiction.

Similarly ST_If vs ST_IfTrue/False: ST_If requires t1 --> t1' where t1 = true, contradicting value_no_step (true is value).

So determinism is provable.

Actually, do we even need determinism? Alternative: SN is defined as accessibility; there's a general fact: if all one-step reducts of t are SN then SN t (that's the constructor). So to show SN t from SN t' and t --> t', we need all reducts of t SN. With determinism, all reducts = t'. Without it, we'd need each reduct. Determinism is the easiest path.

Wait, actually maybe we don't need SN t from SN t' for LR_back. Let's look at the use case: In the T_Abs case, we show LR T2 (app (abs T1 t[γ]) u) from LR T2 (open t[γ] u) and the step app --> open. So LR_back is used exactly there. LR_back at type T2 with t = app..., t' = open...:

- SN (app (abs) u): need SN_intro; take t'' with app (abs) u --> t''. By determinism, t'' = open t[γ] u. Then SN t'' from LR T2 (open...) ✓.
- lc (app (abs) u): abs is lc (proven), u is lc (from LR T1 u) ✓.
- Arrow part (if T2 is an arrow): for all value w ∈ LR T2arg: LR T2cod (app (abs) u w). Hmm, we need to derive from LR T2cod (open t[γ] u w)? Wait no, the arrow part of LR at type T2 = A→B says: for all value w ∈ LR A, LR B (app (app (abs u)) w). We don't have anything about open t[γ] u w directly. Hmm, but here's where we use the IH of LR_back at type T2: We're proving LR T2 (app ...) by induction on T2. For T2 = A→B:
  - SN (app (abs) u) as above.
  - lc as above.
  - for all value w ∈ LR A: LR B (app (app (abs) u) w). Good, so LR_back by induction on T with the SN part using determinism.

Alternative standard approach: define the logical relation over *terms* with the arrow condition involving only values, and prove:

Lemma (application): if t1 ∈ R(A→B) and t2 ∈ R(A) then t1 t2 ∈ R(B).

Proof sketch: induction on the multistep reduction of t2 to a value? Since t2 ∈ R(A) implies SN t2, t2 is SN; by a "SN terms of type A reduce to values" fact (progress-ish), t2 -->* v with v a value. This is the standard "R is closed under expansion" for SN relations: if t --> t' and t' ∈ R then t ∈ R. And for multistep, induct.

Let me step back and consider a cleaner, well-known approach for CBV SN: use the "strongly normalizing" relation defined *semantically* as accessibility plus an auxiliary notion, or use the approach where the logical relation is defined over *values* and terms are related if they are SN and their value reducts are related. This is the standard "CIU" or "gluing" approach for CBV.

Common approach for CBV (e.g., in Leroy/CompCert or "normalization by evaluation"): Define:
- V(Bool) = {true, false} (closed values)
- V(A→B) = { λx.t | ∀v ∈ V(A). t[v] ∈ T(B) }
- T(B) = SN terms whose reducts are in V... 
Hmm, but then the fundamental theorem "Γ ⊢ t : T → t ∈ T(T)" requires substitution.

There's a neat known trick for proving SN in CBV: define the relation R(T) on terms as: SN and all reducts are "good", etc. This is like a "step-indexed" or "reduction-closed" relation. Let me think of a definition that makes everything work:

Define LR by recursion on type:
- LR(Bool) t := SN t ∧ lc t.
- LR(A→B) t := SN t ∧ lc t ∧ (∀ u, value u → LR A u → LR B (t u)).

And prove: (1) fundamental theorem with environments (needs the application lemma for arbitrary t2), (2) application lemma via a separate argument.

For the application lemma, maybe we can use the following: given LR (A→B) t1 and LR A t2:

Wait, but for ST_AppAbs: t1 = abs A body with lc; t2 = v value; reduct open body v. We need SN (open body v). Do we know LR B (open body v)? From the fundamental theorem... no, t1's relation doesn't directly give it. Hmm, unless we *define* the relation to include it. This is the crux: for a value t1 = abs A body ∈ R(A→B), we want: for all value v ∈ R A, LR B (open body v). The arrow clause gives LR B (app (abs A body) v), and if we have "LR_back" (expansion closure) then LR B (app...) from LR B (open body v) — backwards. We need the forward direction: from LR B (app (abs) v) derive LR B (open body v)? That's not generally valid (expansion closure goes the wrong way).

Indeed, the standard property: R is closed under reduction (forward): if t ∈ R(T) and t --> t', then t' ∈ R(T). Let's check: for our definition, does it hold? By induction on T:
- Bool: SN t → SN t' ✓ (SN_intro gives all reducts SN). lc preserved (need lemma).
- Arrow A→B: Need SN t' ✓, lc t' ✓, and for all value u ∈ R A: LR B (t' u). Given LR B (t u) (from R t) and t u --> t' u by ST_App1 (needs lc u ✓ from LR A u → lc). So LR B (t' u) by IH at B ✓. 

So LR_step holds. And the abstraction case: R(A→B) (abs A body) requires for all value v ∈ R A: LR B (app (abs A body) v). We prove this by deriving LR B (open body v) — from where? We need the fundamental theorem-ish statement for the body.

Hmm OK here's the thing: the abstraction case of the fundamental theorem gives us: for all value v ∈ R A, LR B (subst (γ, x:=v) (open body (fvar x))) = LR B (open body[γ] v) — this comes from the IH of the fundamental theorem applied at type B to the opened body, with the *extended environment*. That's fine! It's exactly the LR B (open body[γ] v) we need. Then we get LR B (app (abs A body[γ]) v) via LR_back (expansion closure) with ST_AppAbs (needs value v ✓ and lc (abs A body[γ]) ✓). 

So the abstraction case doesn't need the application lemma at all. The application lemma is only needed for T_App: given LR (A→B) t1 and LR A t2 (t2 arbitrary, possibly not a value), show LR B (app t1 t2).

Let me instead design the proof to sidestep the issue. Key idea: use the "hereditary" relation with an extra clause capturing application to any related term, and prove the needed properties by *nested induction on type and SN*. Perhaps define:

LR T t := match T with
  | Bool => SN t ∧ lc t
  | A→B => SN t ∧ lc t ∧ (∀ u, LR A u → LR B (t u))
  end

Note: no value restriction! Then:
- Application lemma: immediate by definition (arrow clause) for t1 ∈ R(A→B): for all u ∈ R A, LR B (t1 u) ✓ — that's exactly clause 3. So T_App case is trivial!
- Fundamental theorem T_Abs case: need to show for t = abs A body (subst γ body): SN ✓ (no reducts, wait — no! With the unrestricted relation, SN of the abstraction itself is still fine: abstraction has no steps... yes since step's redexes are app/if only). lc ✓ (need body lc). And for all u ∈ R A (arbitrary u, not just values): LR B (app (abs A body[γ]) u).

That's the hard part now: for arbitrary u ∈ R A, show LR B (app (abs A body') u).

Let's analyze: u ∈ R(A) implies SN u, lc u. We want LR B (app (abs A body') u).

Case B = Bool: need SN (app (abs) u), lc ✓. SN: by induction on SN u? Steps of app (abs) u:
  - ST_AppAbs if value u: reduct open body' u, need SN. From the IH of the fundamental theorem at value u? We have LR B (open body' u) for value u ∈ R A (by the fundamental theorem IH with extended environment, x fresh, γ[x:=u]) — for value u ✓ (this is available since we can instantiate with value u). Wait, but does the fundamental theorem's IH apply to *arbitrary* u? The environment extension γ[x:=u] requires env_ok: needs lc u ✓ and LR A u ✓ — no value requirement. So we get LR B (open body' u) for the value case ✓.
  - ST_App2: u --> u', reduct app (abs) u'. Need SN of that; by induction on SN u (u' is a reduct, so SN u'), using the full statement "for all u ∈ R A, LR B (app (abs) u)" — but u' ∈ R A? u ∈ R A → u' ∈ R A by LR_step (forward closure) ✓! And SN u' from SN u ✓. So induction on SN u works if the goal is LR B (app (abs) u) with all the structure... but in the ST_App2 case we need just SN (app (abs) u') for B=Bool ✓ from IH.

Case B = C→D: need SN, lc, and for all w ∈ R C: LR D (app (app (abs) u) w). Hmm. Now we need a strategy for the arrow part. Note app (app (abs) u) w. Hmm, the induction hypothesis at u' (a reduct of u) with the SAME w: LR B (app (abs) u') gives its arrow part: LR D (app (app (abs) u') w). Then app (app (abs) u) w --> app (app (abs) u') w by ST_App1 (inner app steps by ST_App2 with value abs ✓, then ST_App1 needs lc w ✓). So by LR_back ✓. Lemma LR_stuck: forall T t, lc t -> (forall t', ~ (t --> t')) -> LR T t.
Proof: induction T.

Similarly, LR_abs_app: induction on SN u. Let me restate the plan with all lemmas:

Setting: tm, subst, lc, step, value, has_type given.

Definitions:
```
Definition lc_env (gamma : atom -> tm) : Prop := forall x, lc_at 0 (gamma x).
Definition update_tm (gamma : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (u : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else gamma y.
Definition env_ok (Gamma : context) (gamma : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  lc_env gamma /\ (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> LR T (gamma x)).
```

Wait, but LR is defined by Fixpoint over ty; env_ok mentions LR, so order: define LR first, then env_ok. But LR's definition doesn't mention env_ok ✓.

LR definition:
```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t /\ lc_at 0 t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => strongly_normalizing t /\ lc_at 0 t /\
      (forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```
Note: the recursive call `LR T1 u` in the body of LR at Ty_Arrow T1 T2 — structurally smaller T1 ✓ and T2 ✓, so the guard checker should accept.

```
  intros A B body Hlc Hprem u Hu.
  destruct (Hu) as [HSNu]? -- LR A u gives SN u.
  induction HSNu as [u Hu_step IH].
```

- Else:

Alternatively, we could prove these facts not by deciding but by eliminating with a "SN value or step" lemma: For any term t, either t is a value, or t steps, or t is stuck-nonvalue-and-no-step — this is a tautology requiring excluded middle on `value t` and `exists t', t --> t'`. Let's just prove decidability. We need:
- `ty_eq_dec : forall (T1 T2 : ty), {T1 = T2} + {T1 <> T2}` (for tm equality, since abs contains ty).

- `value_dec : forall t, {value t} + {~ value t}` using lc_at_dec.
- `step_dec`? For "exists t', t --> t'" we can decide by a boolean/function since step is decidable; but maybe we don't need full step decidability: we need, for a given term, either it steps or not.   t = bvar/fvar/true/false/abs: no step (left? no — the second: forall t', ~ step).

  t = if t1 t2 t3: steps if t1 = true (with lc t2, lc t3), t1 = false, or t1 steps [ST_If].

Hmm, let me look at this from the angle: how do standard Coq developments prove this? I recall the "stlc" strong normalization in the "metalib" or in "Charguéraud's tutorial" uses the following trick for CBV: they define the logical relation on *closed values* and prove that every well-typed term reduces to a value... Actually here's a cleaner known approach for CBV SN:

Define SN, then define:
```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Bool => SN t
  | A -> B => SN t /\ forall v, value v -> LR A v -> LR B (t v)
  end.
```
Then prove:
1. LR implies SN, lc.
2. LR is closed under forward and backward reduction.
3. If t1 ∈ LR(A→B), t2 ∈ LR(A), t2 SN then t1 t2 ∈ LR(B) — by induction on SN t2 with the "value or step" case analysis? Hmm, still needs the case analysis, but maybe with a cleverer measure: 

Lemma LR_app: LR (A→B) t1 -> LR A t2 -> LR B (t1 t2).
Proof: induction on SN t2? We need: t2 SN. Hmm.

Alternatively use the "evaluation" approach: any SN term t2 has a normal form? In CBV STLC, a closed well-typed term is either a value or steps; but arbitrary LR terms (like stuck) are normal forms that aren't values...

Wait, actually, maybe the stuck case can be handled uniformly: If t2 has no reducts, then t1 t2's reducts are: ST_AppAbs (if t2 value), ST_App1 (t1 steps). You know what? Let's just do the decidability lemmas. They're mechanical and make the proof constructive and clean. Actually, hmm, there might be an even simpler approach: since `step` is a deterministic relation and the language is small, we can define a *function* `step_fun : tm -> option tm` that computes the reduct, and prove `step t t' <-> step_fun t = Some t'`. Then "no reducts" is `step_fun t = None`, decidable by computation! That's elegant:

```
Fixpoint step_fun (t : tm) : option tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_app t1 t2 =>
      match t1 with
      | tm_abs T t0 => match t2 with ... if value t2 then Some (open t0 t2) else ... 
      ...
```
Hmm, but value is a Prop involving lc_at. We need to decide value anyway. So define `lcb : nat -> tm -> bool` and `valueb : tm -> bool`, prove correctness (lcb k t = true ↔ lc_at k t; valueb t = true ↔ value t). Then step_fun. Then prove step_fun_spec: step t t' ↔ step_fun t = Some t'. This gives determinism for free and decidability of "has a step". But the correctness proof still requires work. Hmm, but it's clean.

Actually — wait. Let me reconsider. Maybe we can avoid decidability altogether by reformulating the lemmas to *not* need the stuck case:

Observation: In LR_abs_app, the case "u is not a value and has no reducts" leads to app (abs) u being stuck. Instead of proving LR B (app (abs) u) via LR_stuck, note that the *premise* "forall u, LR A u -> LR B (open body u)" doesn't apply. Hmm.

Define LR T t := SN t ∧ lc t ∧ ... plus a "not stuck" condition? The issue: LR T must be closed under forward and backward reduction, and the fundamental theorem must produce it.

Progress: closed well-typed terms are values or step. In the fundamental theorem, terms subst γ t are closed (no fvars) if γ maps to closed terms and Γ's domain covers t's fvars. Since γ maps each typed var to a closed value (in LR), we get closedness. Then progress gives value-or-step. But the terms of the fundamental theorem are typed? We'd need a substitution lemma for typing: Γ ⊢ t : T, γ maps x:T to closed terms u with ⊢ u : T (in empty context) ⟹ ⊢ subst γ t : T. This is a standard but nontrivial lemma (substitution preserves typing, with the locally nameless opening). Hmm, this doubles the work.

Hmm, hold on. Let's think about what clause to add to LR to exclude stuck terms while remaining provable:
LR Bool t := SN t ∧ lc t ∧ (value t ∨ (exists t', t --> t')). Hmm, for a term like app true true (stuck, not a value): excluded ✓. For terms like `app (abs Bool (bvar 0)) true` ∈ LR Bool: SN ✓, lc ✓, steps ✓.

I think decidable value/step is the cleanest path. Let's do it with functions:

Define:
```
Fixpoint lcb (k : nat) (t : tm) : bool :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => Nat.ltb i k
  | tm_fvar _ => true
  | tm_app t1 t2 => andb (lcb k t1) (lcb k t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => lcb (S k) t1
  | tm_true => true
  | tm_false => true
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => andb (lcb k t1) (andb (lcb k t2) (lcb k t3))
  end.

Lemma lcb_spec : forall k t, lcb k t = true <-> lc_at k t.
```
Prove by induction on t. Then `valueb`:
```
Definition valueb (t : tm) : bool :=
  match t with
  | tm_abs T t1 => lcb 1 t1   (* lc_at 0 (abs T t1) = lc_at 1 t1 *)
  | tm_true => true
  | tm_false => true
  | _ => false
  end.
Lemma valueb_spec : forall t, valueb t = true <-> value t.
```
✓.

Then step_fun:
```
Fixpoint step_fun (t : tm) : option tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_app t1 t2 =>
      match t1, t2 with
      | tm_abs T t0, _ => if valueb t2 then Some (open t0 t2)
                          else match step_fun t2 with Some t2' => Some (tm_app t1 t2') | None => None end
      | _, _ => match step_fun t1 with
                | Some t1' => if lcb 0 t2 then Some (tm_app t1' t2) else None
                | None => if valueb t1 then match step_fun t2 with Some t2' => Some (tm_app t1 t2') | None => None end
                          else None
                end
      end
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 =>
      if lcb 0 t2 then if lcb 0 t3 then
        match t1 with
        | tm_true => Some t2
        | tm_false => Some t3
        | _ => match step_fun t1 with Some t1' => Some (tm_if t1' t2 t3) | None => None end
        end
      else None else None
  | _ => None
  end.
```
Hmm, careful: the guard checker needs structural recursion: step_fun t2 where t2 is a subterm of t via the match on t1/t2... When matching on t1 (a subterm of t = tm_app t1 t2), `step_fun t2` is on t2 which is a subterm of t, but is it structurally decreasing? The recursive call must be on a variable obtained by pattern matching from t. t2 is a direct subterm ✓. But inside `match t1, t2 with ...` where t1 and t2 are the match-bound variables from the outer match — Coq's guard checker should accept step_fun t2 and step_fun t1 (they're subterms of the original t). Actually to be safe, write nested matches:
```
  match t with
  | tm_app t1 t2 =>
      match t1 with
      | tm_abs T t0 =>
          if valueb t2 then Some (open t0 t2)
          else match step_fun t2 with Some t2' => Some (tm_app t1 t2') | None => None end
      | _ =>
          match step_fun t1 with
          | Some t1' => if lcb 0 t2 then Some (tm_app t1' t2) else None
          | None => if valueb t1 then
                      match step_fun t2 with Some t2' => Some (tm_app t1 t2') | None => None
                    else None
          end
      end
  | ...
```
Hmm, for t1 = abs but t2 not a value and t2 doesn't step: could ST_App1 apply (abs steps)? No, abs doesn't step. So None ✓. But careful: if t1 = abs and t2 is not a value and steps, ST_App2 applies ✓.

Wait, but also: if t1 = abs T t0 and valueb t2 false and step_fun t2 = Some t2' ✓ handled.

For the general case t1 not an abs: if t1 steps: ST_App1 requires lc t2; so if lcb 0 t2 false → None (and indeed no rule applies: ST_App1 blocked; ST_App2 requires value t1 — could t1 be a value and also step? no; if t1 steps then t1 not a value... hmm, value true/false don't step, abs doesn't step. So if step_fun t1 = Some, t1 is not a value ✓ so ST_App2 doesn't apply; ST_AppAbs requires t1 = abs ✗). So the "if lcb 0 t2 then Some ... else None" is right; actually if ST_App1's premise t1 --> t1' holds and lc t2 fails, no rule gives a step ✓.
If t1 doesn't step: ST_App1 doesn't apply; if t1 is a value, ST_App2 applies when t2 steps ✓; if t1 not a value, no steps ✓. And ST_AppAbs requires t1 = abs ✗ (since t1 not abs). ✓.

Then prove `step_fun_spec : forall t t', step t t' <-> step_fun t = Some t'`. This requires induction on t (or on the step derivation) with inversion. This is doable but somewhat tedious; also gives determinism as a corollary:
`step_det : step t t1 -> step t t2 -> t1 = t2` from step_fun_spec ✓.
And `step_dec : forall t, { t' | step t t' } + { forall t', ~ step t t' }`:
```
destruct (step_fun t) as [t'|] eqn:E.
- left; exists t'; apply step_fun_spec; rewrite E; reflexivity.
- right; intros t' H; apply step_fun_spec in H; rewrite E in H; discriminate.
```
✓ Easy!

And value_dec from valueb_spec.

Hmm, but proving step_fun_spec requires care: the direction step → step_fun = Some t' by induction on the step derivation; and the direction step_fun = Some → step by induction on t. Both need inversion and case analysis. Let's estimate: 6 step rules and 7 term constructors. Manageable.

Actually, do we even need the full step_fun_spec? We need:
(a) determinism (for LR_back SN part),
(b) decidability of "has a step" (for the stuck case),
(c) possibly `value_dec`.

Alternatively, we can get determinism by a direct proof (as I outlined) and decidability of step by a direct decision procedure... The step_fun approach gives both at once. But the step_fun_spec proof might be comparable in effort to direct determinism + direct step_dec. Hmm. Let's think about which is less error-prone. I think step_fun + spec is reasonably clean, and the spec proof by induction on t with case analysis is mechanical: for each constructor, show step t t' ↔ step_fun t = Some t'. Actually, `rewrite` with if_then_else and valueb_spec/lcb_spec should make it manageable. But the `if valueb t2` inside step_fun requires case analysis `destruct (valueb t2) eqn:E` and using valueb_spec to know value/non-value.

Alternatively, prove determinism directly (6 cases, using value_no_step), and prove "step_dec" via the step_fun? Circular—step_dec needs step_fun anyway.

Hmm, do we actually need full "step_dec"? We need, for a term t with SN t: either value t, or ∃t', t --> t', or (¬value t ∧ ∀t', ¬ t --> t'). Actually we need it in the specific form for the lemmas; maybe we can prove a trichotomy lemma:

```
Lemma value_or_step_or_stuck : forall t,
  value t \/ (exists t', t --> t') \/ (~ value t /\ forall t', ~ (t --> t')).
```
This requires deciding value t and ∃t'. With step_dec and value_dec, trivial.

So the plan: build valueb/lcb/step_fun with specs. Let's estimate the proof work: 
- lcb_spec: induction on t, ~20 lines.

OK let's go with the functional approach. But hmm, wait: maybe there's an even simpler way to get the trichotomy: We can prove it by well-foundedness? No.

Hmm, actually, here's another thought: maybe we can avoid the case analysis entirely by strengthening LR_abs_app's premise to cover the stuck case. The premise from the fundamental theorem is "forall u, LR A u -> LR B (open body u)". In the stuck case, we want LR B (app (abs A body) u) where u is stuck. Note app (abs A body) u is stuck, so LR B (app (abs A body) u) should hold as long as B is any type — via LR_stuck. So the question is just how to *detect* that we're in the stuck case. What if LR_abs_app is proven by *strong induction on the SN derivation of u* where the induction motive is exactly "LR B (app (abs A body) u)" — with the case analysis on u's shape done *syntactically* by destructing u? 

Idea: destruct u by cases (tm_bvar, tm_fvar, tm_app, tm_abs, true, false, tm_if). For each constructor, determine whether value/step/stuck *syntactically*! Because whether a term is a value or steps is largely determined by its head constructor:
- tm_bvar i: not a value; no step (step never applies to bvar) → stuck ✓ (need lc ✓).
- tm_fvar x: not a value; no step → stuck ✓.
- tm_true: value ✓.
- tm_false: value ✓.
- tm_abs T body: value iff lc — we have lc from LR A u ✓ → value ✓.
- tm_app t1 t2: not a value; steps iff ... need recursion.
- tm_if t1 t2 t3: not a value; steps iff ... need recursion.

So destructing u gives partial info; for app/if we need to know whether they step. Recursion needed. Hmm. This suggests an auxiliary recursive decision on terms — i.e., step_dec again.

OK, decision procedures it is. Let's think about the cleanest implementation. Actually, maybe simpler than step_fun: prove `step_dec` directly by induction on the term using the structure:

```
Lemma step_dec : forall t, { t' | t --> t' } + { forall t', ~ (t --> t') }.
```
Hmm, the tricky part: for app t1 t2, we need to decide whether t1 is an abs AND value t2 → ST_AppAbs; or t1 steps (needs lc t2); or value t1 and t2 steps. The negative branch requires showing all rules blocked. This is essentially the same as step_fun.

I'll do step_fun approach but let me think about whether the spec proof is really needed in full generality. We need:
1. `step_fun t = Some t'` → `t --> t'` (soundness) — used for step_dec.
2. `t --> t'` → `step_fun t = Some t'` (completeness) — used for step_dec and determinism.

We could prove completeness by induction on the step derivation (6 cases, each computing step_fun for the specific shape — simpl should reduce; needs valueb_spec/lcb_spec to resolve the ifs). Soundness by induction on t (7 cases; for each, analyze step_fun's branches, using valueb_spec/lcb_spec, and construct the step).

```
Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => []
  | tm_fvar x => [x]
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => []
  | tm_false => []
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => fv t1 ++ fv t2 ++ fv t3
  end.
```

Let me instead write the lemma as:
```
Lemma LR_abs_app : forall A B body u,
  lc_at 0 (tm_abs A body) ->
  (forall u, LR A u -> LR B (open body u)) ->
  LR A u -> LR B (tm_app (tm_abs A body) u).
Proof.
  intros A B body u Hlc Hprem Hu.
  assert (HSN : strongly_normalizing u) by apply (LR_SN _ _ Hu).
  revert Hu.
  induction HSN as [u Hred IH].
  intros Hu.
  ...
```

Now, let me reconsider the overall proof of the main theorem:
```
Theorem normalization : forall t T, <{ empty |-- t \in T }> -> strongly_normalizing t.
Proof.
  intros t T Hty.
  assert (H : LR T (subst (fun x => tm_fvar x) t)).
  { apply fundamental with (Gamma := empty) (gamma := fun x => tm_fvar x).
    - split.
      + intro x; simpl; constructor.  (* lc_fvar *)
      + intros x T' Hx; unfold empty in Hx; discriminate. }
  rewrite subst_id in H.
  apply LR_SN in H; exact H.
Qed.
```
✓.

Now let's think about potential difficulties in the guard checker for the LR Fixpoint and about unfolding/simpl behavior.

LR definition:
```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t /\ lc_at 0 t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\ lc_at 0 t /\
      (forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```
This should be accepted by the guard checker (recursive calls on T1, T2 which are subterms of T) ✓.

Hmm, one concern: `LR T1 u` appears inside a forall — positive occurrence, fine for Fixpoint guard checking (it's a Prop, and the guard condition only cares about recursive calls on smaller arguments) ✓.

Simplification: `simpl LR` or `unfold LR` in proofs. Since LR is a Fixpoint on T, `simpl` with T a concrete type reduces ✓; with T a variable, it doesn't reduce. In lemmas by induction on T, `destruct T as [| A B]` then `simpl` unfolds ✓. But `simpl in H` where H : LR (Ty_Arrow A B) t should reduce ✓. However, careful: `simpl` may unfold LR everywhere and produce messy terms; `unfold LR; fold LR` might be needed. Usually `simpl in *` works OK.

Since LR is a Fixpoint with two arguments (T and t), and the recursion is on T, `simpl` should unfold when T is a constructor ✓.

Another consideration: the mutually recursive behavior — LR T1 u where T1 is a subterm: in a proof by induction on T, we get IHs for T1 and T2 ✓.

I think the step_fun approach is cleaner overall. Let's define:

Actually, alternative: define step_fun but also prove the spec via a "view" lemma. Let me write out the step_fun definition carefully.

```
Fixpoint lcb (k : nat) (t : tm) : bool :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => Nat.ltb i k
  | tm_fvar _ => true
  | tm_app t1 t2 => andb (lcb k t1) (lcb k t2)
  | tm_abs _ t1 => lcb (S k) t1
  | tm_true => true
  | tm_false => true
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => andb (lcb k t1) (andb (lcb k t2) (lcb k t3))
  end.
```
Need `Nat.ltb` and spec: `Nat.ltb_lt : forall n m, (n <? m) = true <-> n < m` ✓ (in PeanoNat, maybe named Nat.ltb_lt). Also `Nat.eqb_eq` ✓. `andb` requires Bool (from Stdlib.Init.Bool or Bool.Bool). With `From Stdlib Require Import Arith.PeanoNat`, `andb` might be available via Init.Datatypes? `andb` is in Coq.Init.Datatypes or Bool. Let me just Require Import Bool.Bool too. Actually, `Nat.leb`, `Nat.ltb` are in PeanoNat ✓.

valueb:
```
Definition valueb (t : tm) : bool :=
  match t with
  | tm_abs _ t1 => lcb 1 t1
  | tm_true => true
  | tm_false => true
  | _ => false
  end.
```
Spec: valueb t = true <-> value t.
- abs: valueb = lcb 1 t1; value (tm_abs T t1) ↔ lc_at 0 (tm_abs T t1) ↔ lc_at 1 t1 ✓.
- true/false ✓.
- others: valueb = false; value (bvar/app/if) is False by inversion ✓.

step_fun:
```
Fixpoint step_fun (t : tm) : option tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_app t1 t2 =>
      match t1 with
      | tm_abs T t0 =>
          if valueb t2 then Some (open t0 t2)
          else match step_fun t2 with
               | Some t2' => Some (tm_app t1 t2')
               | None => None
               end
      | _ =>
          match step_fun t1 with
          | Some t1' => if lcb 0 t2 then Some (tm_app t1' t2) else None
          | None => if valueb t1 then
                      match step_fun t2 with
                      | Some t2' => Some (tm_app t1 t2')
                      | None => None
                      end
                    else None
          end
      end
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 =>
      match t1 with
      | tm_true => if lcb 0 t2 then if lcb 0 t3 then Some t2 else None else None
      | tm_false => if lcb 0 t2 then if lcb 0 t3 then Some t3 else None else None
      | _ => match step_fun t1 with
             | Some t1' => if lcb 0 t2 then if lcb 0 t3 then Some (tm_if t1' t2 t3) else None else None
             | None => None
             end
      end
  | _ => None
  end.
```
Wait: for ST_If, the premises are (t1 --> t1'), lc t2, lc t3. So if t1 steps and lc t2, lc t3 → step ✓. If t1 steps but lc t2 fails → no rule ✓ (ST_If blocked; ST_IfTrue requires t1 = true but t1 steps so not a value... hmm, if t1 = true then t1 doesn't step, so we're in the other branch). In the `_` branch (t1 not true/false), ST_IfTrue/False can't apply (they need syntactic true/false). Hmm, careful: the match is on the syntax of t1; `_` covers abs/bvar/fvar/app/if. ST_IfTrue requires t1 = tm_true exactly ✓ excluded. So in `_` branch only ST_If applies ✓. But also: if t1 = tm_true and lc t2 fails: branch tm_true → None; ST_IfTrue needs lc t1=t2 and lc t2... premises lc t1 (which is tm_true ✓) and lc t2 (fails) → blocked ✓; ST_If requires true --> t1' → false ✓. So None ✓.

But hold on: the guard checker for step_fun: `match t1 with tm_abs T t0 => ... step_fun t2 ...` — step_fun t2 where t2 is a variable bound in the outer match on t = tm_app t1 t2. The recursive call on t2: is t2 recognized as a subterm of t? In Coq's guard condition, `t2` is the pattern variable from the match on `t`, so it's a strict subterm ✓. Similarly t1. And in the `tm_if` branch, step_fun t1 where t1 is a subterm ✓. Should be fine.

Hmm, one issue: in the `tm_abs` branch, I use `Some (tm_app t1 t2')` where t1 is the matched variable — t1 = tm_abs T t0 but t1 is a variable bound in `match t with tm_app t1 t2`. Fine.

```
Lemma step_fun_sound : forall t t', step_fun t = Some t' -> t --> t'.
```

Let me restructure the app case:
```
| tm_app t1 t2 =>
    match t1 with

    | _ => ...
```
Hmm, but careful: if the abs is not lc, ST_App2 also requires value v1 = value (abs T t0) = lc_at 0 (abs) = lcb 1 t0 → blocked. So None ✓.

Similarly for the `_` branch: valueb t1 checks value t1 which handles lc for abs (but t1 isn't abs in this branch; for true/false it's fine).

Hmm wait, in the `_` branch, t1 could be tm_true/tm_false (values) or a non-value. `valueb t1` handles that ✓.

Let me re-examine the `tm_abs` branch when lcb 1 t0 = true and valueb t2 = false: we check step_fun t2 → ST_App2 ✓ (value v1 = abs needs lc ✓, t2 --> t2' ✓). If t2 doesn't step: no rules ✓.

Now, also: when t1 is an abs with lcb 1 t0 = true and valueb t2 = true: ST_AppAbs ✓. But ALSO ST_App2 would need t2 --> t2', but t2 is a value so no ✓.

OK, the function is:
```
Fixpoint step_fun (t : tm) : option tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_app t1 t2 =>
      match t1 with
      | tm_abs T t0 =>
          if lcb 1 t0 then
            if valueb t2 then Some (open t0 t2)
            else match step_fun t2 with Some t2' => Some (tm_app t1 t2') | None => None end
          else None
      | _ =>
          match step_fun t1 with
          | Some t1' => if lcb 0 t2 then Some (tm_app t1' t2) else None
          | None => if valueb t1 then
                      match step_fun t2 with Some t2' => Some (tm_app t1 t2') | None => None
                    else None
          end
      end
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 =>
      match t1 with
      | tm_true => if lcb 0 t2 then if lcb 0 t3 then Some t2 else None else None
      | tm_false => if lcb 0 t2 then if lcb 0 t3 then Some t3 else None else None
      | _ => match step_fun t1 with
             | Some t1' => if lcb 0 t2 then if lcb 0 t3 then Some (tm_if t1' t2 t3) else None else None
             | None => None
             end
      end
  | _ => None
  end.
```
Hmm, one more check in the `_` branch of app: t1 not an abs. If step_fun t1 = Some t1' and lcb 0 t2: ST_App1 ✓. Could ST_App2 also apply? It needs value t1; if t1 steps then t1 is not a value (values don't step) ✓ so no conflict — but the function returns a single value; determinism ensures they'd be equal anyway. But we need soundness: with step_fun t1 = Some t1' and lcb 0 t2, is there a step? ST_App1 ✓.
If step_fun t1 = None and valueb t1 = true and step_fun t2 = Some t2': ST_App2 ✓.
If step_fun t1 = None, valueb t1 = false: no rules: ST_App1 needs t1 --> ✗; ST_App2 needs value t1 ✗; ST_AppAbs needs t1 = abs ✗ (in `_` branch) ✓.
If step_fun t1 = None, valueb t1 = true, step_fun t2 = None: ST_App2 needs t2 --> ✗ ✓; ST_App1 needs t1 --> ✗ ✓; ST_AppAbs ✗ ✓. ✓

Completeness (step → step_fun = Some): induction on the step derivation:
- ST_AppAbs: t = app (abs T t0) v; step_fun (app (abs T t0) v) = match... = if lcb 1 t0 then (if valueb v then Some (open t0 v) else ...) — we know lc (abs T t0) → lcb 1 t0 = true (from lcb_spec) and value v → valueb v = true ✓. So = Some (open t0 v) ✓.
- ST_App1: t = app t1 t2 with t1 --> t1', lc t2. Need step_fun (app t1 t2) = Some (app t1' t2). IH: step_fun t1 = Some t1'. Case t1 = abs T t0? But t1 --> t1' and abs doesn't step → contradiction (value_no_step with v_abs). So t1 is not an abs; but syntactically, step_fun's match on t1 needs the `_` branch: we must destruct t1 to know. Since t1 --> t1', t1 can't be an abs (also can't be bvar/fvar/true/false). So destruct t1: cases bvar/fvar/true/false/abs impossible (by inversion on the step? we need value_no_step for true/false/abs, and no step rules for bvar/fvar). Then app/if cases: `_` branch: step_fun t1 = Some t1' ✓ and lcb 0 t2 = true (from lc t2 and lcb_spec) → result Some (app t1' t2) ✓.
- ST_App2: t = app v1 t2 with value v1, t2 --> t2'. step_fun: match v1. If v1 = abs T t0: lcb 1 t0 = true (value gives lc) — then `if valueb t2`: t2 --> t2' means t2 is not a value → valueb t2 = false; then match step_fun t2 = Some t2' (IH) → Some (app v1 t2') ✓. If v1 = true/false: `_` branch: step_fun v1 = None (simpl), valueb v1 = true, match step_fun t2 = Some t2' → Some (app v1 t2') ✓. (v1 can't be app/if/bvar/fvar since value.)
- ST_IfTrue / ST_IfFalse: straightforward with lcb_spec.
- ST_If: t = if t1 t2 t3, t1 --> t1', lc t2, lc t3. Destruct t1: if it's true/false, contradiction (value_no_step); others go to `_` branch: step_fun t1 = Some t1' (IH), lcb 0 t2 = lcb 0 t3 = true → Some (if t1' t2 t3) ✓.

Both directions need destructing t1 in some cases. Manageable.

Then:
```
Lemma step_fun_spec : forall t t', (step_fun t = Some t') <-> (t --> t').
```
from sound + complete.

Corollaries:
```
Lemma step_det : forall t t1 t2, t --> t1 -> t --> t2 -> t1 = t2.
Proof.
  intros t t1 t2 H1 H2.
  apply step_fun_complete in H1. apply step_fun_complete in H2.
  rewrite H1 in H2. injection H2. auto.
Qed.

Lemma step_dec : forall t, { t' | t --> t' } + { forall t', ~ (t --> t') }.
Proof.
  intro t. destruct (step_fun t) as [t'|] eqn:E.
  - left; exists t'; apply step_fun_sound; exact E.
  - right; intros t' H; apply step_fun_complete in H; rewrite E in H; discriminate.
Qed.
```

And value_dec from valueb.

I'll use explicit naming to be safe:

Actually, in Coq, `induction H` on `H : has_type Gamma t T` with a forall goal creates subgoals where the constructor's arguments are introduced with automatic names (H, H0, ...) and IHs named IH... Since it's auto-named, I'd better not rely on names; instead use tactics that refer to goals. Alternatively, use the `induction H as [...]` intro patterns to name things explicitly. The T_Abs constructor has 6 arguments: L, Gamma, T1, T2, t1, and the premise (a forall). So:

`induction H as [Gamma x T Hlookup | L Gamma T1 T2 t1 Hbody IHbody | Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2 Hty1 IH1 Hty2 IH2 | Gamma | Gamma | Gamma t1 t2 t3 T Hty1 IH1 Hty2 IH2 Hty3 IH3]`

Hmm, the exact naming pattern depends on how Coq decomposes the premise `forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type ...` into IH. With the intro pattern `[L Gamma T1 T2 t1 Hbody IHbody]`, Coq will try to introduce the last argument (the premise) as Hbody and its IH as IHbody. Since the premise is a forall-type, the IH should be automatically generated... Let me recall: for an inductive premise of the form `forall x, P x`, the induction principle has `(forall x, P x)` as a single premise with IH `(forall x, IH_of_P x)`. In the tactic, `induction H as [ ... | L Gamma T1 T2 t1 Hbody IHbody | ...]` — hmm, when the premise is a forall/arrow type, Coq's intro pattern for the induction principle must account for the IH being nested. Actually the generated induction principle for has_type:

```
has_type_ind :
  forall P : context -> tm -> ty -> Prop,
  (forall Gamma x T, Gamma x = Some T -> P Gamma (tm_fvar x) T) ->
  (forall L Gamma T1 T2 t1,
      (forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2) ->
      (forall x, ~ In x L -> P (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2) ->
      P Gamma (tm_abs T1 t1) (T1 -> T2)) ->
  ...
```
Yes, Coq generates two premises: the original and the IH version. So the intro pattern for T_Abs: `L Gamma T1 T2 t1 Hbody IHbody` ✓ (Hbody the original forall-premise, IHbody the IH forall-premise).

Hmm, is that right? For an argument of type `forall x, A x -> B x`, Coq's induction principle splits into the argument and its IH, with the IH being `forall x, A x -> P ...` — yes, standard.

OK. But there's a subtlety: When the premise is a forall, does Coq automatically generate the IH through it? I believe yes: e.g., for `Inductive foo := c : (forall x, foo) -> foo`, the induction principle is `forall P, (forall x, P) -> P` hmm, that's weird (x not appearing). For our case, the premise's conclusion is has_type ..., which P applies to ✓, so the IH is `forall x, ~ In x L -> P (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2` ✓.

To be safe, I'll write the induction with explicit intro patterns and check the errors. Actually, safer approach: use `remember`/`induction H` without patterns and rely on auto-generated names, then inspect goals via compilation errors. Iterative development will sort it out.

Alternatively, I can avoid the naming issue by proving the fundamental theorem via `induction H` and using `match goal with` — no, let's just iterate.

Let me write `env_ok_update`:
```
Lemma env_ok_update : forall Gamma gamma x T u,
  env_ok Gamma gamma -> LR T u -> env_ok (update Gamma x T) (update_tm gamma x u).
Proof.
  intros Gamma gamma x T u [Hlc Hty] Hu. split.
  - intros y. unfold update_tm. destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E.
    + apply LR_lc. exact Hu.
    + apply Hlc.
  - intros y T' Hy. unfold update_tm. destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst y.
      unfold update in Hy. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl in Hy. injection Hy as Hy. subst T'. exact Hu.
    + apply Hty with (x := y). unfold update. rewrite E. exact Hy.
Qed.
```
Careful: `unfold update in Hy` — update is `fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then Some T else Gamma y`; after `unfold update`, Hy : (if Nat.eqb x y then Some T else Gamma y) = Some T'. Then subst y := x, rewrite Nat.eqb_refl, Hy : Some T = Some T', injection → T = T' ✓.

Hmm, note the naming: `T` is the type in the update and `T'` the looked-up type. Fine.

```
  intros T. induction T; intros t H; simpl in H; apply (proj1 H).
```

```
Lemma LR_lc : forall T t, LR T t -> lc_at 0 t.
Proof.
  intros T. induction T; intros t H; simpl in H.
  - apply (proj2 H).
  - apply (proj1 (proj2 H)).
Qed.
```

One consideration on `fresh`: define
```
Fixpoint max_list (l : list atom) : atom :=
  match l with
  | nil => 0
  | x :: l' => Nat.max x (max_list l')
  end.

Lemma max_list_spec : forall l x, In x l -> x <= max_list l.
Proof.
  intros l x H. induction l as [| y l' IH].
  - inversion H.
  - simpl. apply in_inv in H. destruct H as [-> | H].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + apply Nat.le_trans with (max_list l'); [apply IH; exact H | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Definition fresh (l : list atom) : atom := S (max_list l).

Lemma fresh_not_in : forall l, ~ In (fresh l) l.
Proof.
  intros l H. apply max_list_spec in H. unfold fresh in H. lia.
Qed.
```

```
Fixpoint step_fun (t : tm) : option tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_app t1 t2 =>
      match t1 with

                 | None => None
                 end

          match step_fun t1 with

              if lcb 0 t2 then Some (tm_app t1' t2) else None
          | None =>
              if valueb t1 then
                match step_fun t2 with
                | Some t2' => Some (tm_app t1 t2')
                | None => None
                end
              else None
          end

      match t1 with
      | tm_true =>
          if lcb 0 t2 then (if lcb 0 t3 then Some t2 else None) else None
      | tm_false =>
          if lcb 0 t2 then (if lcb 0 t3 then Some t3 else None) else None
      | _ =>
          match step_fun t1 with
          | Some t1' =>
              if lcb 0 t2 then (if lcb 0 t3 then Some (tm_if t1' t2 t3) else None) else None
          | None => None
          end
      end
  | _ => None
  end.
```

Let me write the code text.

```coq
(** * Helpers for the strong normalization proof. *)

(** ** Fresh atoms and free variables *)

Fixpoint max_list (l : list atom) : atom :=
  match l with
  | nil => 0
  | x :: l' => Nat.max x (max_list l')
  end.

Lemma max_list_spec : forall (l : list atom) (x : atom), In x l -> x <= max_list l.
Proof.
  intros l x H. induction l as [| y l' IH].
  - inversion H.
  - simpl in H. destruct H as [-> | H].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + apply Nat.le_trans with (max_list l'); [apply IH; exact H | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Definition fresh (l : list atom) : atom := S (max_list l).

Lemma fresh_not_in : forall l, ~ In (fresh l) l.
Proof.
  intros l H. apply max_list_spec in H. unfold fresh in H. lia.
Qed.

Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => fv t1 ++ fv t2 ++ fv t3
  end.
```

```coq
Fixpoint lcb (k : nat) (t : tm) : bool :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => Nat.ltb i k
```

Part 1 infrastructure (fresh, fv, lcb, valueb, value lemmas) compiles. Now let's add the step function and its specification.

Let me now write the step_fun section.

```coq
(** *** A computable step function. *)

Fixpoint step_fun (t : tm) : option tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_app t1 t2 =>
      match t1 with
      | tm_abs _ t0 =>
          if lcb 1 t0 then
            if valueb t2 then Some (open t0 t2)
            else match step_fun t2 with
                 | Some t2' => Some (tm_app t1 t2')
                 | None => None
                 end
          else None
      | _ =>
          match step_fun t1 with
          | Some t1' =>
              if lcb 0 t2 then Some (tm_app t1' t2) else None
          | None =>
              if valueb t1 then
                match step_fun t2 with
                | Some t2' => Some (tm_app t1 t2')
                | None => None
                end
              else None
          end
      end
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 =>
      match t1 with
      | tm_true =>
          if lcb 0 t2 then (if lcb 0 t3 then Some t2 else None) else None
      | tm_false =>
          if lcb 0 t2 then (if lcb 0 t3 then Some t3 else None) else None
      | _ =>
          match step_fun t1 with
          | Some t1' =>
              if lcb 0 t2 then (if lcb 0 t3 then Some (tm_if t1' t2 t3) else None) else None
          | None => None
          end
      end
  | _ => None
  end.
```

Then soundness and completeness. Let me think again about the exact tactical structure.

```
    intros t' H; simpl in H.
  - discriminate H.   (* bvar *)
  - discriminate H.   (* fvar *)
  - (* app *) ...
  - discriminate H.   (* abs: step_fun (abs) = match tm_abs ... => ? -- hmm! *)
```

(4) tm_abs T1 e1: H : (if lcb 1 e1 then if valueb a2 then Some (open e1 a2) else match step_fun a2 with ... end else None) = Some t'.

   Same as the app case: destruct step_fun, lcb 0 a2, then ST_App1 with IH.

destruct c1 as [...]; simpl in H for each:
- tm_true: destruct (lcb 0 c2), (lcb 0 c3), injection → ST_IfTrue ✓.
- tm_false: → ST_IfFalse ✓.

Completeness:
```
Lemma step_fun_complete : forall t t', t --> t' -> step_fun t = Some t'.
Proof.
  intros t t' H. induction H.
  - (* ST_AppAbs *) simpl.
    assert (Hlc : lc_at 1 t) by (inversion H; assumption).
    rewrite (proj2 (lcb_spec t 1) Hlc).
    rewrite (proj2 (valueb_spec v) H0).
```

```
Lemma step_det : forall t t1 t2, t --> t1 -> t --> t2 -> t1 = t2.
Proof.
  intros t t1 t2 H1 H2.
  apply step_fun_complete in H1. apply step_fun_complete in H2.
  rewrite H1 in H2. injection H2. auto.
Qed.

Lemma step_dec : forall t, { t' | t --> t' } + { forall t', ~ (t --> t') }.
Proof.
  intro t. destruct (step_fun t) as [t'|] eqn:E.
  - left. exists t'. apply step_fun_sound. exact E.
  - right. intros t' H. apply step_fun_complete in H. rewrite E in H. discriminate H.
Qed.

Lemma value_dec : forall t, { value t } + { ~ value t }.
Proof.
  intro t. destruct (valueb t) eqn:E.
  - left. apply (proj1 (valueb_spec t)). exact E.
  - right. intro H. apply (proj2 (valueb_spec t)) in H. rewrite H in E. discriminate E.
Qed.
```

Hmm, what about this: define a *predicate* characterizing "normal" terms:

Inductive normal : tm -> Prop :=
  | n_bvar : forall i, normal (bvar i)
  | n_fvar : forall x, normal (fvar x)
  | n_true : normal true
  | n_false : normal false
  | n_abs : forall T t, lc_at 0 (abs T t) -> normal (abs T t)   (* values are normal *)
  | n_app : forall t1 t2, normal t1 -> normal t2 -> ~ value t1 -> normal (app t1 t2)
  | n_if : forall t1 t2 t3, normal t1 -> ~ value t1 -> normal (if t1 t2 t3)

and prove: normal t → (∀t', ¬ t --> t'), and normal t ↔ (no step ∧ not a value ...)? Hmm, we'd still need to decide whether a term is normal, which is the same problem.

OK, let me think about the real root issue: we need to decide, for a given term u, whether it steps. Since step is a simple syntactic relation, maybe we can prove the decision lemma by induction on u where the recursive calls are on *subterms*, and the negative cases are handled by inversion. The combinatorial explosion I feared might not be that bad because the negative cases are handled uniformly by inversion on the (impossible) step hypothesis. Let me think:

Lemma step_dec : forall t, {t' | t --> t'} + {forall t', ~ t --> t'}.
Proof by induction on t:
- bvar/fvar/true/false/abs: right; intros t' H; inversion H.  ✓ (5 easy cases)
- app t1 t2: 
  destruct (value_dec t2) — hmm, value_dec needs lcb. Fine, prove value_dec first.
  Hmm, but actually we need to decide several things:
  - Is t1 an abs with lc, and value t2? → ST_AppAbs.
  - Does t1 step and lc t2? → ST_App1.
  - Is value t1 and does t2 step? → ST_App2.
  Note these overlap only in impossible ways. Structure:
  destruct t1 as [...] (7 cases):
  * t1 = bvar/fvar: ST_AppAbs impossible; ST_App1 needs t1 --> (impossible by inversion); ST_App2 needs value t1 (impossible: bvar/fvar not values). So right; intros; inversion H (all cases contradictory: ST_AppAbs requires tm_abs = bvar; ST_App1 gives a step for bvar — impossible by inversion on that; ST_App2 requires value (bvar) — inversion). This requires some inversion work but doable.
  * t1 = app d1 d2: 
    - IH1 : step_dec (app d1 d2) gives either a step or no step.
      + if step: decide lcb 0 t2 (decidable): if lc t2 → left (ST_App1); else right (ST_App1 blocked; ST_AppAbs blocked since t1 not abs; ST_App2 requires value (app) impossible) ✓.
      + if no step: then t1 doesn't step. Is t1 a value? app is not a value ✓. So no rules apply: ST_AppAbs ✗ (not abs), ST_App1 ✗ (no step), ST_App2 needs value t1 ✗. → right ✓.
    - Actually here we don't even need value_dec!
  * t1 = if ...: same as app.
  * t1 = true/false: value; ST_AppAbs ✗ (not abs); ST_App1 ✗ (true doesn't step); ST_App2: value true and t2 steps → decide step_dec t2: if step → left (ST_App2); else right ✓.
  * t1 = abs T0 b0: ST_App1 ✗ (abs doesn't step); ST_App2: value (abs) iff lc_at 1 b0 — decidable via lcb: if lcb 1 b0 = true then value (abs) and if t2 steps → ST_App2; ST_AppAbs: if lc and value t2 → ST_AppAbs. Combine: if lcb 1 b0: if value_dec t2 → ST_AppAbs; else decide step_dec t2 → ST_App2 or right; if ¬lcb: right.

Actually — wait. Here's a MUCH simpler idea for step_dec based on the observation that we can decide stepping by checking the *shape*:

Since the step rules only fire when:
- app (abs T body) v with lc, value v
- app t1 t2 with t1 --> t1' and lc t2
- app v1 t2 with value v1 and t2 --> t2'

Alternatively, define a *relation-free* characterization of "no step" by structural inversion:

Lemma no_step_app : forall t1 t2, (forall s, ~ (t1 --> s)) -> ~ value t1 -> (forall s, ~ (t2 --> s)) -> forall s, ~ (tm_app t1 t2 --> s).
This is a general lemma: if t1 doesn't step and isn't a value (so t1 isn't an abs... wait, an abs is a value if lc; if ~value t1 then t1 isn't an abs with lc; but t1 could be an abs WITHOUT lc: then ST_AppAbs blocked ✓, ST_App1 blocked (no step) ✓, ST_App2 blocked (value t1 false) ✓). So:
```
Lemma no_step_app : forall t1 t2,
  (forall s, ~ (t1 --> s)) ->
  ~ value t1 ->
  (forall s, ~ (t2 --> s)) ->
  (forall s, ~ (tm_app t1 t2 --> s)).
Proof.
  intros t1 t2 H1 Hnv1 H2 s H.
  inversion H; subst.
  - (* ST_AppAbs: t1 = tm_abs T t0 and value v where v = t2 -- gives value t2, and t1 = abs => value t1 (v_abs with lc) contradicts Hnv1 *)
  ...
```
Hmm, inversion on H : tm_app t1 t2 --> s where t1 t2 are variables: as discussed, inversion will unify each constructor conclusion with tm_app t1 t2, yielding equations like t1 = tm_abs T t0, t2 = v. Then:
- ST_AppAbs case: we get H : locally_closed (tm_abs T t0), H0 : value v = value t2, and after substitution t1 = tm_abs T t0. We need a contradiction with Hnv1 : ~ value t1. But after inversion, t1 is replaced by tm_abs T t0; Hnv1 : ~ value (tm_abs T t0); we can apply it with v_abs H (the lc premise) ✓.
- ST_App1 case: gives t1 --> t1' — contradict H1 ✓.
- ST_App2 case: gives value v1 where v1 = t1 → contradict Hnv1 ✓.
So this lemma proves a fully general "no step" rule for applications, given the three conditions. Similarly:
```
Lemma no_step_if : forall t1 t2 t3,
  (forall s, ~ (t1 --> s)) -> ~ value t1 -> t1 <> tm_true -> t1 <> tm_false -> ...
```
Hmm, for the if: the rules are ST_IfTrue (t1 = true, lc t2, lc t3), ST_IfFalse, ST_If (t1 steps). If t1 doesn't step and isn't true/false syntactically, no rule applies. Note "~ value t1" isn't needed; we need t1 ≠ true and t1 ≠ false.

Then the decision procedure can be structured as:

Lemma step_dec : forall t, {t' | t --> t'} + {forall t', ~ t --> t'}.
Proof by induction on t:
- bvar/fvar/true/false/abs: right; intros s H; inversion H. (For abs, inversion: no rule concludes with an abs LHS ✓; for bvar/fvar/true/false similarly ✓.)
- app t1 t2:
  destruct (step_dec t1) as [[s1 H1] | H1].
  + (* t1 steps *)
    destruct (lcb 0 t2) eqn:E.
    * left; exists (tm_app s1 t2); apply ST_App1; [exact H1 | apply lcb_spec; exact E].
    * right; intros s H; apply no_step_app? Hmm, in this case t1 DOES step, so no_step_app doesn't apply. Need to show no rule: ST_App1 requires lc t2 (fails ✓); ST_AppAbs requires t1 = abs — but t1 steps so t1 isn't an abs (abs never steps: prove a lemma `abs_no_step`) ✓; ST_App2 requires value t1 — t1 steps so not a value (value_no_step) ✓.
      So: right; intros s H; inversion H; subst; 
        - ST_AppAbs: t1 = tm_abs T t0 → contradiction with the step H1 (abs doesn't step).
        - ST_App1: premise t1 --> t1' ... plus locally_closed t2; we have lcb 0 t2 = false → ~ lc_at 0 t2 (lcb_spec) contradicting the premise ✓.
        - ST_App2: value t1 contradicts value_no_step H1 ✓.
  + (* t1 doesn't step *)
    destruct (value_dec t1) as [Hv1 | Hnv1].
    * (* t1 is a value *)
      destruct (step_dec t2) as [[s2 H2] | H2].
      -- left; exists (tm_app t1 s2); apply ST_App2; [exact Hv1 | exact H2].
      -- right; intros s H; inversion H; subst.
         ++ ST_AppAbs: gives t1 = tm_abs T t0, value v = value t2... wait, ST_AppAbs's conclusion is app (abs T t) v, so we need t1 = abs and t2 = v. Then the premises: lc (abs) and value v = value t2. But we have H2 : forall s, ~ (t2 --> s) — no contradiction there! Hmm, wait: if t1 is an abs and t2 is a value, ST_AppAbs applies! But we assumed t1 doesn't step and t2 doesn't step. So this case should have been caught: when t1 is a value abs and value t2... The decision procedure must check ST_AppAbs! 
      
      I forgot: if t1 is a value and t2 is a value (so t2 doesn't step), then ST_AppAbs applies when t1 is an abs (with lc). So in the branch "t1 value, t2 no step", I need to check whether t1 is an abs: destruct t1:
        - abs T0 b0: if lcb 1 b0 (lc) and value t2 → left with ST_AppAbs; else right.
        - true/false: no rule (app true t2 stuck) → right.
        - others: not values.
      So the "t1 is a value" branch needs destruction of t1 to check abs.
    * (* t1 is not a value and doesn't step *) right via no_step_app ✓.
    
So the app case: destruct (step_dec t1); destruct (lcb 0 t2); ...; and if t1 doesn't step, destruct (value_dec t1); if value, destruct t1 to check abs; etc. The combinatorial structure is manageable if I organize it well.

Let me reorganize the app case more cleverly:

For t = app t1 t2, the applicable rule is determined by:
- t1 = abs T0 b0 with lcb 1 b0 = true: then if value t2 → ST_AppAbs; else if t2 steps → ST_App2; else no step (ST_App1 doesn't apply since abs doesn't step; ST_App2 needs t2 step).
- t1 ≠ abs (syntactically? no: t1 = abs but ¬lcb): then if t1 steps (impossible for abs; for others) and lc t2 → ST_App1; if value t1 (which for abs requires lc → contrad) and t2 steps → ST_App2; else no step.

So a clean way: destruct t1 into 7 syntactic cases:
1. t1 = bvar i: no value, no step (inversion). So the app's steps: ST_AppAbs ✗ (not abs), ST_App1 ✗ (bvar no step), ST_App2 ✗ (not value) → right; proofs: inversion on the step hypothesis; each case contradicts.
2. t1 = fvar x: same.
3. t1 = app d1 d2: 
   - destruct (step_dec (app d1 d2)) as [[s1 H1] | H1].
     + destruct (lcb 0 t2): left ST_App1 / right (ST_App1 blocked by lc; ST_AppAbs blocked since app isn't abs; ST_App2 blocked since value (app) is impossible).
     + (* no step *) right: ST_App1 ✗; ST_AppAbs ✗; ST_App2 ✗ (value (app) impossible).
4. t1 = if ...: same as 3.
5. t1 = true: not an abs; no step; value ✓. So: destruct (step_dec t2): if step → left ST_App2; else right (ST_AppAbs ✗ not abs; ST_App1 ✗ true doesn't step; ST_App2 needs t2 step ✗).
6. t1 = false: same.
7. t1 = abs T0 b0: destruct (lcb 1 b0) eqn:E.
   + lc: destruct (value_dec t2): if value → left ST_AppAbs; else destruct (step_dec t2): if step → left ST_App2 (value abs ✓); else right (ST_AppAbs needs value t2 ✗; ST_App1 abs doesn't step; ST_App2 needs t2 step ✗).
   + not lc: right (ST_AppAbs needs lc ✗; ST_App1 abs doesn't step ✗; ST_App2 needs value abs = lc ✗).

The "right" branches need inversion proofs. Let me write them using a helper lemma per shape or with explicit inversion on the hypothesis and `subst`.

But careful: `step_dec` is a recursive function over terms, so its proof by induction on t works, and each recursive call is on a strict subterm ✓. The "right" branches are proofs of `forall s, ~ (tm_app X t2 --> s)` where X is a concrete term possibly with variables. `inversion H` should handle the shape mismatch and use the given no-step hypotheses.

```

- (* ST_AppAbs *) ... 
```
Hmm, inversion H where H : tm_app (tm_bvar i) t2 --> s. The constructors:

- ST_App1: conclusion tm_app t1 t2 with premise t1 --> t1'; unifies with t1 := tm_bvar i, t2 := t2; premise: tm_bvar i --> t1' — this is an impossible step, but inversion doesn't automatically know. That's a robust pattern! Let me define a tactic:
```
Ltac solve_no_step :=
  intros s H; inversion H; subst;
  try match goal with H' : _ --> _ |- _ => inversion H' end;
  try match goal with H' : value _ |- _ => inversion H' end;
  try discriminate.
```

Let me revisit the proof structure and see whether the stuck cases can be handled by a *weaker* decision: In LR_abs_app and LR_if, the case analysis is: value u / u steps / u stuck. What if we restructure these lemmas to avoid the third case by *including the disjunction in the LR definition*? Earlier I considered adding "value t ∨ ∃t', t --> t'" to LR and worried about forward closure. But maybe forward closure is not needed if the clause is phrased as: for all reducts... Hmm.

Alternatively: add to LR the clause `value t ∨ ∃ t', t --> t' ∨ (∀ t', ¬ t --> t' ∧ ¬ value t)`. That's a tautology given decidability... not helpful.

Hmm, what if we add to LR the clause "∀ t', t --> t' → (value t' ∨ ∃ t'', t' --> t'')"? That's the progress-along-reducts condition. Let me examine this more carefully, call it P(t): for all t', t --> t' → (value t' ∨ ∃ t'', t' --> t''). Note P is *not* required of t itself. Forward closure: if LR includes SN, lc, P, then LR_step: t --> t', need P(t'): for all t'', t' --> t'' → ...; from P(t) applied to t --> t' we get value t' ∨ ∃t''... hmm, P(t') says for every reduct of t': either value or has a reduct. From P(t) applied to the step t --> t' we get: value t' ∨ ∃t''. t' --> t''. That's not P(t'). But P(t') follows from P(t): any t'' with t' --> t'' — do we know t'' is a value or steps? We need P at t'': since t' --> t'' and P(t) gives... no. Hmm, P(t) only constrains one-step reducts of t.

Define instead Q(t) := for all t', t -->* t' → (value t' ∨ ∃ t'', t' --> t''). Then Q is forward-closed (multistep transitive) ✓. And if t is stuck (no reducts, not value), Q(t) fails at t' = t ✓ — so stuck terms are excluded ✓.

Now, would LR with Q be provable for abstractions and applications? In LR_abs_app: for u ∈ LR A, we know Q(u): u is a value or u steps (take t' = u with multi_refl ✓). So we can destruct the disjunction — no decision procedure needed! ✓ 

And the stuck case never arises. But then we must prove Q for all the terms constructed:
- Q(app (abs A body) u) when u is a value: reducts of the app: beta reduct open body u (and further multisteps). Q holds if... we need: every multistep reduct of app (abs) u is a value or steps. Reducts: open body u itself and its reducts. Hmm, this requires knowing that open body u is in LR B (we have it via Hprem ✓ if u is a value) and LR includes Q (by induction on the LR definition, LR B (open body u) includes Q ✓) plus the beta step giving multistep ✓. So Q(app) follows: take any t' with app -->* t': either zero steps (t' = app: app is not a value; does it step? yes if u value: beta ✓ so ∃ step ✓); or app --> r -->* t': r = open body u (the only one-step reduct when u is a value... plus? ST_App2 needs value abs ✓ and u --> u' but u is a value ✗; ST_App1 abs ✗). So r = open body u and then t' is a multistep reduct of open body u ∈ LR B ⟹ Q(open body u) gives value t' ∨ step ✓.
  This requires reasoning about multisteps and uniqueness of one-step reducts — using determinism again! (Reducts of the app: exactly one.) Hmm, we need: if app --> r then r = open body u — that's determinism-ish (only one rule applies). Actually it's just inversion: the steps from app (abs) v with v value: ST_AppAbs gives open body v; ST_App2 needs v --> ✗; ST_App1 needs abs --> ✗. So the only step is the beta ✓ — provable by inversion without full determinism.
  
  Then multistep decomposition: app -->* t'. If t' = app (refl) → app steps (beta) → ∃ step ✓. Else app --> r -->* t'; r = open body u; LR B (open body u) gives Q → value t' ∨ step ✓.
  
  Hmm, but this argument requires a lemma: multi step case analysis.

This is also getting heavy, and we'd need to prove Q for all LR elements (in the definition) — the fundamental theorem would need to establish Q for every constructed term, which means for the T_App case: Q(app t1 t2) given LR (A→B) t1 and LR A t2. That requires analyzing the evaluation of app t1 t2: if t1 is a value or steps... again case analysis on t1, t2! Argh. Hmm, we need both determinism and step_dec. Determinism can be proven directly by induction on the step derivation, or from the step function. Alternatively, we can prove determinism *and* decidability in one go with a "computation" lemma:

Lemma step_compute : forall t, match step_fun t with
  | Some t' => t --> t' /\ (forall t'', t --> t'' -> t'' = t')
  | None => forall t'', ~ (t --> t'')
  end.

This combines soundness, completeness (determinism follows), and decidability! And it can be proven by induction on t with case analysis. The proof structure is like step_dec but each branch also proves determinism of the result. Let me estimate determinism's proof:

Lemma step_det : forall t t1, t --> t1 -> forall t2, t --> t2 -> t1 = t2.
Proof by induction on the step derivation (generalizing t2). Cases:
- ST_AppAbs T t v Hlc Hv: t1 = open t v, t = tm_app (tm_abs T t) v. Given H2 : tm_app (tm_abs T t) v --> t2, inversion H2; subst:
  - ST_AppAbs with T', t', v': equations give (abs T t) = (abs T' t'), v = v'; then t2 = open t' v' = open t v ✓ (with subst).
  - ST_App1: premise (tm_abs T t) --> t1' — contradict value_no_step (v_abs Hlc) ✓.
  - ST_App2: premise value v1 with v1 = tm_abs T t ✓ fine, and v --> t2' — but Hv : value v, contradict value_no_step Hv ✓.
- ST_App1 t1 t1' t2 Hstep IH Hlc: t = app t1 t2, t1_result = app t1' t2. Given H2 : app t1 t2 --> t2'. inversion; subst:
  - ST_AppAbs: requires t1 = tm_abs T t0 — then Hstep : tm_abs T t0 --> t1' — contradict abs_no_step ✓.
  - ST_App1: t1 --> t1'' with t2' = app t1'' t2; apply IH Hstep to get t1'' = t1' ✓.
  - ST_App2: requires value v1 = value t1 — contradict value_no_step Hstep ✓.
- ST_App2 v1 t2 t2' Hv Hstep IH: t = app v1 t2. Given H2 : app v1 t2 --> s; inversion:
  - ST_AppAbs: requires v1 = tm_abs T t0 and value v (where v = t2) and conclusion s = open t0 t2. But then t2 is a value, contradicting Hstep : t2 --> t2' via value_no_step ✓.
  - ST_App1: requires v1 --> t1' — contradict value_no_step Hv ✓.
  - ST_App2: v1 = v1, t2 --> t2'' and s = app v1 t2''; apply IH to Hstep and the new step ✓.
- ST_IfTrue t1 t2 Hlc1 Hlc2: t = if true t1 t2, result t1. Given H2 : if true t1 t2 --> s; inversion:
  - ST_IfTrue: s = t1 ✓ (maybe with equations).
  - ST_IfFalse: requires true = false ✗ discriminate.
  - ST_If: premise true --> t1' ✗ value_no_step ✓.
- ST_IfFalse similar.
- ST_If t1 t1' t2 t3 Hstep IH Hlc2 Hlc3: t = if t1 t2 t3, result if t1' t2 t3. Given H2; inversion:
  - ST_IfTrue: requires t1 = tm_true → step true --> t1' contradict ✓.
  - ST_IfFalse ✓.
  - ST_If: t1 --> t1''; apply IH ✓.

This is very doable (~50 lines). Good — determinism direct.

```coq
(** *** Basic facts about values and applications/conditionals. *)

Lemma valueb_true : forall t, valueb t = true -> value t.
Proof. intros t H. apply (proj1 (valueb_spec t)). exact H. Qed.

Lemma valueb_false : forall t, valueb t = false -> ~ value t.
Proof.
  intros t H Hv. apply (proj2 (valueb_spec t)) in Hv. rewrite Hv in H. discriminate H.
Qed.

Lemma lcb_true : forall t k, lcb k t = true -> lc_at k t.
Proof. intros t k H. apply (proj1 (lcb_spec t k)). exact H. Qed.

Lemma lcb_false : forall t k, lcb k t = false -> ~ lc_at k t.
Proof.
  intros t k H Hc. apply (proj2 (lcb_spec t k)) in Hc. rewrite Hc in H. discriminate H.
Qed.

Lemma value_dec : forall t, { value t } + { ~ value t }.
Proof.
  intro t. destruct (valueb t) eqn:E.
  - left. apply valueb_true. exact E.
  - right. apply valueb_false. exact E.
Qed.

Lemma step_abs : forall T t s, ~ (tm_abs T t --> s).
Proof. intros T t s H; inversion H. Qed.
Lemma step_bvar : forall i s, ~ (tm_bvar i --> s).
Proof. intros i s H; inversion H. Qed.
Lemma step_fvar : forall x s, ~ (tm_fvar x --> s).
Proof. intros x s H; inversion H. Qed.
Lemma step_true : forall s, ~ (tm_true --> s).
Proof. intros s H; inversion H. Qed.
Lemma step_false : forall s, ~ (tm_false --> s).
Proof. intros s H; inversion H. Qed.

Lemma value_app : forall t1 t2, ~ value (tm_app t1 t2).
Proof. intros t1 t2 H; inversion H. Qed.
Lemma value_if : forall t1 t2 t3, ~ value (tm_if t1 t2 t3).
Proof. intros t1 t2 t3 H; inversion H. Qed.
```

```
Ltac solve_nostep :=
  exfalso;
  first [
      (* value vs step *)
      match goal with
      | Hv : value ?v, Hs : ?v --> ?w |- _ => exact (value_no_step v Hv w Hs)
      end
    | match goal with
      | H : tm_abs ?T ?b --> ?w |- _ => exact (step_abs T b w H)
      end
    | match goal with
      | H : tm_true --> ?w |- _ => exact (step_true w H)
      end
    | match goal with
      | H : tm_false --> ?w |- _ => exact (step_false w H)
      end
    | match goal with
      | H : tm_bvar ?i --> ?w |- _ => exact (step_bvar i w H)
      end
    | match goal with
      | H : tm_fvar ?x --> ?w |- _ => exact (step_fvar x w H)
      end
    | match goal with
      | Hn : ~ lc_at ?k ?t, Hc : lc_at ?k ?t |- _ => exact (Hn Hc)
      end
    | match goal with
      | Hn : ~ value ?v, Hv : value ?v |- _ => exact (Hn Hv)
      end
    | discriminate
    ].
```

Note: in a `match goal with | Hv : value ?v, Hs : ?v --> ?w |- _ => ...`, multiple hypotheses patterns are allowed separated by commas ✓, and both are bound in order ✓.

Important: pattern `?v --> ?w` where v is bound — Ltac allows referring to previously bound metavariables in subsequent patterns (they must match the same term) ✓.

```
    | match goal with
```
```
      | Hs : tm_fvar ?x --> ?w |- _ => exact (step_fvar x w Hs)

    ].
```

```
Lemma step_det : forall t t1, t --> t1 -> forall t2, t --> t2 -> t1 = t2.
Proof.
  intros t t1 H. induction H.
  - (* ST_AppAbs *) intros s Hs. inversion Hs; subst.
    + reflexivity.
    + exfalso. match goal with H' : tm_abs ?T ?b --> ?w |- _ => exact (step_abs T b w H') end.
    + exfalso. match goal with Hv : value ?v, H' : ?v --> ?w |- _ => exact (value_no_step v Hv w H') end.
  - (* ST_App1 *) ...
```
Hmm, let me think about the case structure and what inversion produces.

Case ST_AppAbs (T, t, v, Hlc, Hv): goal `forall t2, tm_app (tm_abs T t) v --> t2 -> open t v = t2`. `intros s Hs; inversion Hs; subst.`
- ST_AppAbs case: s = open t' v' with equations tm_abs T' t' = tm_abs T t and v' = v. After subst, goal `open t v = open t v` → reflexivity ✓.
- ST_App1 case: premise tm_abs T t --> t1' → contradiction via step_abs ✓.
- ST_App2 case: premise value v1 (with v1 = tm_abs T t) and v --> t2' → contradiction via value_no_step Hv ✓.

Case ST_App1 (t1, t1', t2, Hstep, Hlc): goal `forall s, tm_app t1 t2 --> s -> tm_app t1' t2 = s`. intros s Hs; inversion Hs; subst.

How to apply: `match goal with H' : t1 --> ?w |- _ => rewrite (IHH w H') end`? Hmm, the IH name. With `induction H` the IH for the ST_App1 premise is auto-named... In Coq, `induction H` names the IH `IHH`? Typically `IHH` if H is the name. Hmm, but the premise here is a hypothesis of the step derivation, and the constructor's recursive argument. The induction principle: `step_ind : forall P, (forall T t v Hlc Hv, P (app (abs T t) v) (open t v)) -> (forall t1 t1' t2 Hstep, P t1 t1' -> forall Hlc, P (app t1 t2) (app t1' t2)) -> ...`. So the IH is for the step premise ✓, named automatically based on the constructor argument names (Hstep) → `IHHstep` or similar.

To be robust, I can name the induction hypotheses via intro patterns. But the pattern must include arguments AND IHs (as we learned). For step, the constructors:
- ST_AppAbs : forall T t v, lc -> value -> step ... → args: T, t, v, Hlc, Hv (5), no IHs.
- ST_App1 : forall t1 t1' t2, step t1 t1' -> lc t2 -> step ... → args t1, t1', t2, then the recursive premise (arg+IH), then Hlc. So the pattern: `t1 t1' t2 Hstep IHHstep Hlc`.
- ST_App2 : forall v1 t2 t2', value v1 -> t2 --> t2' -> ... → args v1, t2, t2', Hval, then recursive (t2 --> t2') + IH: `v1 t2 t2' Hval Hstep IHHstep`.
- ST_IfTrue : forall t1 t2, lc t1 -> lc t2 -> ...: `t1 t2 Hlc1 Hlc2`.
- ST_IfFalse: same.
- ST_If : forall t1 t1' t2 t3, t1 --> t1' -> lc t2 -> lc t3 -> ...: `t1 t1' t2 t3 Hstep IHHstep Hlc2 Hlc3`.

So: `induction H as [T t v Hlc Hv | t1 t1' t2 Hstep IHHstep Hlc | v1 t2 t2' Hv Hstep IHHstep | t1 t2 Hlc1 Hlc2 | t1 t2 Hlc1 Hlc2 | t1 t1' t2 t3 Hstep IHHstep Hlc2 Hlc3]`.

Then in ST_App1: `IHHstep : forall s, t1 --> s -> t1' = s`? Hmm, the motive is `fun a b => forall s, a --> s -> b = s`. So the IH is `forall s, t1 --> s -> t1' = s` ✓.

In the ST_App1 case: H : t1 --> t1'' (after inversion). Then `IHHstep t1'' H : t1' = t1''` → subst → reflexivity ✓.

```
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
```

```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t /\ lc_at 0 t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => strongly_normalizing t /\ lc_at 0 t /\
      (forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.

Definition env_ok (Gamma : context) (gamma : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  lc_env gamma /\ (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> LR T (gamma x)).

Lemma LR_SN, LR_lc, LR_step, LR_back, LR_stuck, LR_abs_app, LR_if, env_ok_update...
```

```
Lemma LR_back : forall T t t', lc_at 0 t -> t --> t' -> LR T t' -> LR T t.
```

```
Lemma LR_abs_app : forall A B body u,
  lc_at 0 (tm_abs A body) ->
  (forall u, LR A u -> LR B (open body u)) ->
  LR A u ->
  LR B (tm_app (tm_abs A body) u).
Proof.
  intros A B body u Hlc Hprem Hu.
  assert (HSN : strongly_normalizing u) by (apply (LR_SN A); exact Hu).
  revert Hu.
  induction HSN as [u Hred IHu]; intros Hu.
  destruct (value_dec u) as [Hv | Hnv].
  - apply LR_back with (t' := open body u).
    + apply lc_app; [exact Hlc | apply LR_lc; exact Hu].
    + apply ST_AppAbs; [exact Hlc | exact Hv].
    + apply Hprem. exact Hu.
  - destruct (step_dec u) as [[u' Hstep] | Hnostep].
    + apply LR_back with (t' := tm_app (tm_abs A body) u').
      * apply lc_app; [exact Hlc | apply LR_lc; exact Hu].
      * apply ST_App2; [apply v_abs; exact Hlc | exact Hstep].
      * apply IHu; [exact Hstep | apply LR_step with u; assumption].

        -- (* ST_App1 *) exfalso. ns_all.
        -- (* ST_App2 *) exfalso. apply Hnostep. ... hmm.
```
